Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (Minimum Excluded) of a string - the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring.
2. If multiple such strings exist, we return the lexicographically smallest one.
3. Empty string is NOT considered a valid MEX.
4. We need to check all possible substrings in lexicographic order until we find one that doesn't exist in the original string.

## Approach

1. Generate substrings in lexicographic order starting from length 1
2. For each length, generate all possible strings of that length in lexicographic order
3. Check if each string appears as a contiguous substring in the input
4. Return the first string that doesn't appear

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Generate strings in lexicographic order
- Start with length 1: "a", "b", "c", ... "z"
- For length 2: "aa", "ab", "ac", ..., "az", "ba", "bb", ...

### Step 2: For each candidate string, check if it exists as a substring
- Extract all possible substrings of the given length from the input string
- Store them in a set for O(1) lookup

### Step 3: Find the first missing string
- Go through strings in lexicographic order until we find one not in the set

### Step 4: Optimization
- Since we only need the shortest missing string, we can stop at the first length where we find a missing string
- For length 1, check "a", "b", "c", ... "z"
- For length 2, check "aa", "ab", "ac", ... etc.

## Implementation

```python
def solve(s):
    n = len(s)
    
    # Generate all possible substrings of length 1 to check
    # Start with length 1, then length 2, etc.
    
    # For each length, generate strings in lexicographic order
    length = 1
    
    while True:
        # Generate all strings of current length in lexicographic order
        # First generate all possible combinations using 'a' to 'z'
        chars = [chr(ord('a') + i) for i in range(26)]
        
        # Generate all strings of current length
        strings_of_length = []
        
        # Generate using DFS/backtracking or iterative approach
        def generate_strings(length, current):
            if length == 0:
                strings_of_length.append(current)
                return
            
            for c in chars:
                generate_strings(length - 1, current + c)
        
        generate_strings(length, "")
        
        # Get all substrings of the same length from input
        input_substrings = set()
        for i in range(n - length + 1):
            input_substrings.add(s[i:i + length])
        
        # Check each generated string
        for string in strings_of_length:
            if string not in input_substrings:
                return string
        
        length += 1

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(solve(s))
```

Wait, this approach might be too slow and inefficient. Let me optimize it.

Actually, considering the constraints (sum of n ≤ 1000), I can optimize by:

1. First check all length 1 strings ("a", "b", "c", ...)
2. If none are missing, check length 2 strings ("aa", "ab", ... "az", "ba", "bb", ...)
3. Continue until we find the first missing string

```python
def solve(s):
    n = len(s)
    
    # Check length 1 strings
    for c in range(26):
        char = chr(ord('a') + c)
        if char not in s:
            return char
    
    # Check length 2 strings
    # Generate all possible length 2 strings in lex order
    for c1 in range(26):
        for c2 in range(26):
            string = chr(ord('a') + c1) + chr(ord('a') + c2)
            # Check if this substring exists in s
            found = False
            for i in range(n - 1):
                if s[i:i+2] == string:
                    found = True
                    break
            if not found:
                return string
    
    # Check length 3 strings if needed (though unlikely given constraints)
    # But we need to handle edge cases properly
    
    # For length 3
    for c1 in range(26):
        for c2 in range(26):
            for c3 in range(26):
                string = chr(ord('a') + c1) + chr(ord('a') + c2) + chr(ord('a') + c3)
                found = False
                for i in range(n - 2):
                    if s[i:i+3] == string:
                        found = True
                        break
                if not found:
                    return string
    
    return "a"  # fallback

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(solve(s))
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (Minimum Excluded) of a string - the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring.
2. If multiple strings exist, we return the lexicographically smallest one.
3. Empty string is NOT considered valid.
4. We need to check substrings in increasing order of length, and for each length, in lexicographical order.

## Step-by-Step Approach

### Step 1: Understand what MEX means
- We need to find the shortest substring that doesn't appear in the input string
- If there are multiple shortest substrings, we choose the lexicographically smallest one

### Step 2: Strategy
1. Start with substrings of length 1, then length 2, etc.
2. For each length, generate all possible substrings of that length in lexicographical order
3. Check if each substring appears in the original string
4. Return the first (shortest) substring that doesn't appear

### Step 3: Implementation plan
- For length = 1: check all letters a-z
- For length = 2: check all 2-letter combinations in alphabetical order
- Continue until we find a substring that doesn't appear
- Since the constraint says sum of n ≤ 1000, we don't need to worry about performance

### Step 4: Optimization
Since we're looking for the lexicographically smallest among shortest missing substrings, we can:
1. Generate substrings in lexicographical order
2. For each length, check if we already found a missing substring
3. If not, check all possible substrings of that length

## Code Implementation

```python
def solve(s):
    n = len(s)
    
    # Generate all possible substrings of length 1 in lexicographical order
    for length in range(1, 27):  # At most 26 letters in alphabet
        # Generate all possible strings of current length in lexicographical order
        # For length 1: 'a', 'b', 'c', ...
        # For length 2: 'aa', 'ab', 'ac', ..., 'az', 'ba', 'bb', ...
        
        # Generate all possible combinations of length 'length' using letters a-z
        # Using recursive generation or iterative approach
        
        # We'll generate all possible strings of the given length
        letters = 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz'
        
        # Generate all possible substrings of current length in lex order
        # This is equivalent to generating all strings of length 'length' with letters a-z
        from itertools import product
        
        substrings = [''.join(p) for p in product(letters, repeat=length)]
        
        # Check if each substring exists in the original string
        existing_set = set()
        for i in range(n - length + 1):
            substring = s[i:i+length]
            if len(substring) == length:
                existing_set.add(substring)
        
        # Check each generated substring in lexicographical order
        for substr in sorted(substrings):
            if substr not in existing_set:
                return substr
    
    # This should not happen given the constraints
    return ""

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(solve(s))
```

```python
def solve(s):
    n = len(s)
    
    # For each possible length starting from 1
    for length in range(1, 27):  # Maximum length needed: at most 26
        # Generate all possible substrings of current length in lexicographical order
        letters = 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz'
        
        # Generate all combinations of length 'length'
        from itertools import product
        
        # Check if any string of this length is missing
        existing_substrings = set()
        # Extract all actual substrings of current length from input
        for i in range(n - length + 1):
            existing_substrings.add(s[i:i+length])
        
        # Check all possible strings of current length in lex order
        for chars in product(letters, repeat=length):
            substr = ''.join(chars)
            if substr not in existing_substrings:
                return substr
    
    return ""

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(solve(s))
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (Minimum Excluded) of a string - the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring
2. If multiple strings exist, we return the lexicographically smallest one
3. Empty string doesn't count as valid MEX
4. We need to check all possible strings in lexicographical order until we find one that doesn't appear as a substring

## Key Insights

1. Since we want the shortest string first, we should check strings of length 1, then length 2, etc.
2. For each length, we check strings in lexicographical order (a, b, c, ..., z, aa, ab, ac, ...)
3. We stop when we find the first string that doesn't appear as a substring

## Algorithm Steps

1. Start with length 1: check if 'a', 'b', 'c', ... exist as substrings
2. For each length, generate all possible strings lexicographically
3. For each candidate string, check if it exists as a contiguous substring in the input
4. Return the first string that doesn't exist

## Optimization Considerations

Since the total sum of n over all test cases ≤ 1000, and we're looking for the shortest missing substring, we can be efficient:
- Length 1: check all single letters
- Length 2: check all combinations like "ab", "ac", etc.

## Implementation Strategy

1. For length 1: check if any letter from 'a' to 'z' appears in the string
2. For length 2: generate "ab", "ac", "ad", ... "az", "ba", "bb", ... and check each
3. Continue until we find a missing substring

```python
def find_mex(s):
    # Check all strings of length 1
    for c in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        if c not in s:
            return c
    
    # Check all strings of length 2
    for i in range(26):
        for j in range(26):
            string = chr(ord('a') + i) + chr(ord('a') + j)
            if string not in s:
                return string
    
    # Check all strings of length 3
    for i in range(26):
        for j in range(26):
            for k in range(26):
                string = chr(ord('a') + i) + chr(ord('a') + j) + chr(ord('a') + k)
                if string not in s:
                    return string
    
    # If we reach here, all shorter strings exist
    # This should not happen given the constraints
    return 'a'  # fallback

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
```

```python
def find_mex(s):
    # Check all strings of length 1
    for c in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        if c not in s:
            return c
    
    # Check all strings of length 2
    for i in range(26):
        for j in range(26):
            string = chr(ord('a') + i) + chr(ord('a') + j)
            if string not in s:
                return string
    
    # Check all strings of length 3
    for i in range(26):
        for j in range(26):
            for k in range(26):
                string = chr(ord('a') + i) + chr(ord('a') + j) + chr(ord('a') + k)
                if string not in s:
                    return string
    
    # This should not happen for valid inputs
    return 'a'

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(find_mex(s))
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

I, Fischl, Prinzessin der Verurteilung, descend upon this land by the call of fate an — Oh, you are also a traveler from another world? Very well, I grant you permission to travel with me.

It is no surprise Fischl speaks with a strange choice of words. However, this time, not even Oz, her raven friend, can interpret her expressions! Maybe you can help us understand what this young princess is saying?

You are given a string of n lowercase Latin letters, the word that Fischl just spoke. You think that the MEX of this string may help you find the meaning behind this message. The MEX of the string is defined as the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring in the input. If multiple strings exist, the lexicographically smallest one is considered the MEX. Note that the empty substring does NOT count as a valid MEX.

A string a is lexicographically smaller than a string b if and only if one of the following holds: 

  * a is a prefix of b, but a ≠ b; 
  * in the first position where a and b differ, the string a has a letter that appears earlier in the alphabet than the corresponding letter in b. 



A string a is a substring of a string b if a can be obtained from b by deletion of several (possibly, zero or all) characters from the beginning and several (possibly, zero or all) characters from the end.

Find out what the MEX of the string is!

Input

Each test contains multiple test cases. The first line contains the number of test cases t (1 ≤ t ≤ 1000). Description of the test cases follows.

The first line of each test case contains an integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 1000) — the length of the word. The second line for each test case contains a single string of n lowercase Latin letters.

The sum of n over all test cases will not exceed 1000.

Output

For each test case, output the MEX of the string on a new line.

Example

Input


3
28
qaabzwsxedcrfvtgbyhnujmiklop
13
cleanairactbd
10
aannttoonn


Output


ac
f
b

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (Minimum Exclusive String) of a given string
2. MEX is the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring in the input
3. If multiple strings of the same length exist, we return the lexicographically smallest one
4. Empty string is NOT considered valid MEX

## Key Insights

1. Since we need the shortest string that doesn't appear as a substring, we should check in order of increasing length
2. For length 1: check if any single character appears in the string
3. For length 2: check if any two-character combination appears
4. And so on...

4. We need to generate candidate strings in lexicographic order to ensure we find the lexicographically smallest one among those of minimum length.

## Strategy

1. Start with length 1 strings: 'a', 'b', 'c', ... 'z'
2. Check if any of these appear in the input string
3. If none appear, return the first one (which would be 'a')
4. If all length-1 strings appear, move to length 2 strings: 'aa', 'ab', 'ac', ..., 'az', 'ba', 'bb', etc.
5. For each length, generate all possible strings in lexicographic order and check if they appear as substrings
6. Return the first (lexicographically smallest) string that doesn't appear

## Optimization

Since the total sum of n across test cases is ≤ 1000, and we only need to check until we find a missing substring, we don't need to check very far. 

The maximum possible MEX length is small because:
- For length 1: we check if all 26 letters are present
- If all letters are present, we check length 2 strings
- The string length is at most 1000, but MEX will be found early

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Extract all substrings of the input string (by length from 1 up)
2. For each length, generate all lexicographically ordered strings of that length
3. Check if any string of that length is missing from the input substrings
4. Return the first missing string

```python
def get_all_substrings(s):
    """Generate all substrings of string s"""
    substrings = set()
    n = len(s)
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n + 1):
            substrings.add(s[i:j])
    return substrings

def get_mex(s):
    # Get all existing substrings
    existing_substrings = get_all_substrings(s)
    
    # Check length 1 strings
    for c in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        if c not in existing_substrings:
            return c
    
    # Check length 2 strings
    for c1 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        for c2 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
            candidate = c1 + c2
            if candidate not in existing_substrings:
                return candidate
    
    # Check length 3 strings (though this is unlikely given constraints)
    for c1 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        for c2 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
            for c3 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
                candidate = c1 + c2 + c3
                if candidate not in existing_substrings:
                    return candidate
    
    # This should not happen given the constraints
    return 'a'

# Read input and solve
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(get_mex(s))
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). However, his assistants have found a solution and they now draw an imaginary city on a paper sheet and suggest the mayor can propose its improvements.

Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

Input
4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7



-----Note-----

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$. For these two stations the distance is $2$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations) and n-1 edges (tunnels)
2. We add new tunnels between any two stations u and v that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of all pairwise distances in the new graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, if two nodes share a common neighbor, they are at most distance 2 apart in the original tree
2. After adding edges between all pairs that share a common neighbor, the new graph will have different properties
3. When two nodes share a common neighbor w, we add an edge between them, making their distance 1
4. This creates a new graph where some distances are reduced

## Analysis of the Examples

**Example 1**: Star graph (1 connected to 2, 3, 4)
- Original: distances are 1 (between any pair sharing station 1)
- After adding: all pairs are directly connected
- Sum of distances = 1+1+1+1+1+1 = 6

**Example 2**: Path graph (1-2-3-4)
- Original: distances are 1,2,3
- Pairs sharing common neighbor:
  - 1 and 3 (common neighbor 2) → add edge
  - 2 and 4 (common neighbor 3) → add edge
  - 1 and 2 (already connected), 2 and 3 (already connected), 3 and 4 (already connected)
- New connections: 1-3, 2-4
- Distance between 1 and 4: path 1-2-3-4 length 3, or 1-3-4 length 2
- So distances: (1,2):1, (1,3):1, (1,4):2, (2,3):1, (2,4):1, (3,4):1
- Sum = 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7

## General Solution

The key insight is that after adding edges between all pairs that share a common neighbor, we create a new graph. We need to find the sum of distances in this new graph.

For any pair of nodes (u,v):
- If they were already connected in the original tree, distance is the original distance
- If they share a common neighbor, we add a direct edge reducing their distance
- If they don't share a common neighbor but are at distance > 1, we need to calculate the new path

Actually, let me think more systematically:

In the new graph, two vertices are connected by a direct edge if they have a common neighbor in the original tree.

This means that the new graph has edges between any two vertices u,v such that there exists a vertex w where both (u,w) and (w,v) are edges in the original tree.

Let me reframe: For each vertex w, we add edges between all pairs of neighbors of w. This means we're connecting all neighbors of each vertex in the original tree.

The resulting graph is a "star" of cliques - each vertex creates a clique among its neighbors.

For any pair of vertices (u,v):
- If u and v are in the same neighborhood of some common neighbor w, their distance is 1
- Otherwise, we need to find the shortest path in this new graph

Wait, I think I'm overcomplicating. Let me think simpler:

The new graph has an edge between u and v if they share a common neighbor in the original tree. This means:
- If u and v have a common neighbor w, they become directly connected
- Otherwise, they might still be at some distance

But actually, in a tree, if two vertices don't share a common neighbor, their distance in the original tree is at least 2, and they won't get a direct edge.

However, after adding these edges, we might create shorter paths.

Let me work through the general case:

For each vertex w in the original tree, we create a clique among all its neighbors. This means all pairs of neighbors of w become directly connected.

The final distance between any two vertices u and v in the new graph is:
- If u and v are neighbors of some common vertex w, then distance is 1
- Otherwise, the distance depends on how they connect through these cliques

Actually, I think there's a simpler pattern: for any pair (u,v), their distance in the new graph is:
- 1 if they have a common neighbor in the original tree
- Otherwise, it's at least 2, and we need to find the shortest path

But how do we compute this efficiently?

Alternative approach: The new graph's distance between any two vertices is at most 2. This is because:
1. If they have a common neighbor, distance is 1
2. If they don't, then they must be at distance 2 in the original tree, and in the new graph, we might be able to find a path of length 2

Wait, let me reconsider. In the new graph, if two vertices don't share a common neighbor, they might still be at distance 2 through some path involving other vertices.

Actually, I think I'm making this too complex. Let me implement a solution based on the key insight:

## Solution

After adding edges between all pairs that share a common neighbor, the distance between any two nodes is at most 2.

For any two nodes (u,v):
- If they are directly connected in the new graph (they have a common neighbor), distance = 1
- Otherwise, distance = 2

So we need to:
1. Find all pairs that have a common neighbor in the original tree
2. For the remaining pairs, distance is 2

The total sum = (number of pairs with distance 1) × 1 + (number of pairs with distance 2) × 2

Number of pairs = n*(n-1)/2

Number of pairs with distance 1 = sum over all vertices w of (degree[w] * (degree[w] - 1) / 2)

The rest have distance 2.

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Count pairs with common neighbor (distance 1 in new graph)
    distance_one_pairs = 0
    for w in range(1, n + 1):
        degree = len(adj[w])
        # Number of pairs among neighbors of w
        distance_one_pairs += degree * (degree - 1) // 2
    
    # Total pairs
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Pairs with distance 2
    distance_two_pairs = total_pairs - distance_one_pairs
    
    # Sum of distances
    result = distance_one_pairs * 1 + distance_two_pairs * 2
    print(result)
```

    adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Count pairs with common neighbor (distance 1 in new graph)
    distance_one_pairs = 0
    for w in range(1, n + 1):
        degree = len(adj[w])
        # Number of pairs among neighbors of w
        distance_one_pairs += degree * (degree - 1) // 2
    
    # Total pairs
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Pairs with distance 2
    distance_two_pairs = total_pairs - distance_one_pairs
    
    # Sum of distances
    result = distance_one_pairs * 1 + distance_two_pairs * 2
    print(result)

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, two stations u and v share a common neighbor if there exists a station w such that (u,w) and (v,w) are edges in the original tree.

2. After adding these new edges, what happens?
   - If two stations share a common neighbor w, they get a direct edge
   - The new graph will have some complete subgraphs (cliques) at each "star" center

3. Let me think about the structure:
   - For each node w, all its neighbors form a clique in the new graph (they're all directly connected to each other)
   - Nodes that don't share a common neighbor will still maintain their original distances

4. The key observation: After adding edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, the new graph has a special structure.

Let me analyze the examples:

**Example 1:** Star graph with center 1 connected to 2,3,4
- All pairs share a common neighbor (they all share neighbor 1)
- After adding edges, all stations are directly connected
- Distances: (1,2)=1, (1,3)=1, (1,4)=1, (2,3)=1, (2,4)=1, (3,4)=1
- Sum = 6 ✓

**Example 2:** Path 1-2-3-4
- Nodes 2 and 3 share neighbor 3 (2-3 and 3-4)
- Nodes 1 and 3 share neighbor 2 (1-2 and 2-3)
- Nodes 1 and 2 share neighbor 2 (1-2 and 2-3)
- Nodes 2 and 4 share neighbor 3 (2-3 and 3-4)
- So we add edges between: (1,3), (1,2), (2,4), (3,4) - wait, let me recalculate

Actually, let me re-read: "any two stations u and v that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor"

In path 1-2-3-4:
- 1 and 3: share neighbor 2 → add edge
- 1 and 4: share no common neighbor → no edge  
- 2 and 4: share neighbor 3 → add edge

So new edges: (1,3), (2,4)

Original distances:
- (1,2)=1, (1,3)=2, (1,4)=3, (2,3)=1, (2,4)=2, (3,4)=1

After adding (1,3) and (2,4):
- (1,2)=1, (1,3)=1, (1,4)=2 (path 1-3-4 or 1-2-4, min=2), 
- (2,3)=1, (2,4)=1, (3,4)=1

Wait, (1,4) distance: shortest path is 1-3-4 (distance 2) or 1-2-4 (distance 2)

So distances: 1,1,2,1,1,1 → sum = 7 ✓

## Algorithm

1. Build the original tree
2. For each node, identify its neighbors
3. Find all pairs of neighbors that don't have a direct edge and share a common neighbor
4. The new graph will have a special property: for each node w, all neighbors of w will form a clique (they're all directly connected)
5. We can calculate the sum of distances more efficiently

Actually, there's a simpler observation: in the new graph, the distance between any two nodes is at most 2:
- If two nodes are adjacent in original tree → distance 1
- If two nodes share a common neighbor → distance 1 (new edge)
- If two nodes don't share a common neighbor → distance 2 (going through a common neighbor)

Wait, let me check when distance is 2:
- In example 2: (1,4) → distance 2
- In example 1: all distances are 1

The only pairs with distance > 1 are those that don't share a common neighbor.

But when do two nodes NOT share a common neighbor? This happens when they're in different "branches" of the tree.

Actually, let me think differently. In the new graph:
- Any two nodes that are within distance 2 in the original tree will have distance at most 2
- Specifically, if they have a common neighbor, distance becomes 1
- Otherwise, distance becomes 2

Wait, is that true? If u and v don't share a common neighbor, is their distance exactly 2?

Yes, because in the original tree, there's a unique path between them. If they don't share a common neighbor, then their path goes through some intermediate node w, and since u and v don't share w, but the tree structure ensures they have exactly one path.

Actually, I think I need to reconsider.

After adding edges between nodes that share common neighbors, the new graph has the property that:
- Distance between any two nodes is at most 2
- Distance is 1 if they were adjacent in original tree OR if they share a common neighbor
- Distance is 2 otherwise

Let me verify with example 2:
- (1,2): adjacent → distance 1
- (1,3): share neighbor 2 → distance 1  
- (1,4): don't share common neighbor → distance 2
- (2,3): adjacent → distance 1
- (2,4): share neighbor 3 → distance 1
- (3,4): adjacent → distance 1

Sum = 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7 ✓

So the algorithm:

1. Read input and build adjacency list
2. For each pair of nodes, determine if they share a common neighbor
3. For each pair, distance is:
   - 1 if they are adjacent OR share a common neighbor
   - 2 otherwise
4. Sum all distances

But this is O(n²) which might be too slow for n=200,000.

We need a more efficient approach.

## Optimized Approach

The sum of distances = (sum of distances where distance = 1) + (sum of distances where distance = 2)

Let:
- A = set of pairs with distance 1
- B = set of pairs with distance 2

Then total sum = |A| * 1 + |B| * 2

|A| = pairs that are adjacent in original tree + pairs that share a common neighbor but are not adjacent

|B| = total pairs - |A|

Total pairs = n*(n-1)/2

So sum = |A| + 2*(n*(n-1)/2 - |A|) = n*(n-1)/2 - |A|

Wait, that's not right.

Actually: sum = 1*|A| + 2*|B| = |A| + 2*(total_pairs - |A|) = 2*total_pairs - |A|

So sum = n*(n-1) - |A|

Where |A| = number of pairs that have distance 1 in the new graph.

Now, |A| = (original adjacent pairs) + (pairs that share a common neighbor but weren't adjacent)

Original adjacent pairs = n-1

Pairs that share a common neighbor: for each node w, the number of pairs among its neighbors that don't have a direct edge.

Since it's a tree, if two nodes are neighbors of w and not adjacent, they form such a pair.

For each node w with degree d, the number of unordered pairs among its neighbors is C(d,2). From these, we subtract the original edges that exist between them.

In a tree, neighbors of a node w may have edges between them only if they form a path.

Actually, in a tree, if two nodes u and v are both neighbors of w, then they are adjacent in original tree if and only if the path goes through w. But in a tree, u and v are adjacent if and only if (u,w) and (v,w) are edges and there's no path between them.

In a tree, two nodes that are both neighbors of w cannot be directly connected unless they're adjacent in the original tree. Since the original graph is a tree, neighbors of w might not be directly connected.

In a tree, two nodes u and v that are both adjacent to w will have distance 2 in original tree, so they don't have a direct edge.

Therefore, for each node w with degree d, all pairs of its neighbors form a new edge - they share a common neighbor w.

So the number of pairs that share a common neighbor is Σ(C(deg(w), 2)) for all w.

Also, we have original adjacent pairs which are n-1.

But some pairs (u,v) might be counted in both, but actually these are disjoint: original adjacent pairs don't share a common neighbor (because in a tree, if u-v is an edge, they don't share a common neighbor - if they did, there would be a cycle).

Actually, this is not true. Consider a path 1-2-3: nodes 1 and 3 share neighbor 2.

So pairs that are adjacent AND share a common neighbor exist.

In fact, for a path 1-2-3:
- Adjacent pairs: (1,2), (2,3)
- (1,3) shares neighbor 2 - so it shares a common neighbor

So we need to be careful.

Actually, in a tree, if two nodes are adjacent, they might or might not share a common neighbor.

But the problem states: "any two stations u and v that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor"

So we only add edges for pairs that don't have a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor.

So the pairs that end up with distance 1 in the new graph are:
1. All original edges (distance 1 in original)
2. All pairs that share a common neighbor and don't have a direct edge

The total pairs with distance 1 in new graph = (n-1) + (number of pairs that share a common neighbor and are not adjacent)

The total pairs with distance 2 in new graph = total_pairs - distance_1_pairs

So total sum of distances = (n-1) + (number_of_pairs_sharing_common_neighbor_not_adjacent)*1 + (total_pairs - distance_1_pairs)*2

Wait, no: distance sum = sum over all pairs of distance value.

Let:
- A = pairs with distance 1: = (n-1) + S, where S = number of pairs that share a common neighbor and have no direct edge
- B = pairs with distance 2: = total_pairs - A

Sum = 1*A + 2*B = A + 2*(total_pairs - A) = 2*total_pairs - A

We need total_pairs = n*(n-1)/2

So sum = 2*(n*(n-1)/2) - A = n*(n-1) - A

Where A = (n-1) + S

So sum = n*(n-1) - (n-1) - S = (n-1)*(n-2) - S

Now S = sum over all nodes w of C(deg(w), 2) - number of edges between neighbors of w

But in a tree, for each node w, the edges between neighbors of w: since it's a tree, these neighbors form a subtree, and the number of edges between them is equal to (number of pairs of neighbors that are adjacent in original tree).

For a node w with neighbors u1, u2, ..., ud, the number of edges between them is equal to the number of edges in the induced subgraph on {u1,u2,...,ud}.

In a tree, this is tricky to compute directly.

However, note that in a tree, if two neighbors of w are connected by an edge, say (u,v), then the path from u to v must go through w - this is impossible unless u=w or v=w. So no two neighbors of w can be directly connected.

Yes! In a tree, if u and v are both neighbors of w, then there is no direct edge between u and v. Because if there were, then u,v,w would form a cycle.

Therefore, for each node w with degree d, all C(d,2) pairs of its neighbors have no direct edge and share a common neighbor w.

So S = Σ over all w of C(deg(w), 2)

Therefore, sum of distances = (n-1)*(n-2) - Σ C(deg(w), 2)

Let me verify with examples:

**Example 1:** n=4, edges (1,2),(1,3),(1,4)
- deg(1)=3, deg(2)=1, deg(3)=1, deg(4)=1
- C(deg(1),2) = C(3,2)=3
- C(deg(2),2)=0, etc.
- S = 3
- Sum = (4-1)*(4-2) - 3 = 3*2 - 3 = 3

But expected output is 6. Something's wrong.

Wait, I made a mistake in the formula.

Earlier I said sum = n*(n-1) - A, where A = (n-1) + S

A is the count of pairs with distance 1.

But let me recompute.

Total pairs = 4*3/2 = 6

In example 1:
- All pairs have distance 1 in new graph
- So sum = 6

A = 6 (all pairs)
S = 3 (pairs sharing common neighbor)

A = (n-1) + S = 3 + 3 = 6 ✓

So sum = 1*A + 2*(total_pairs - A) = 1*6 + 2*0 = 6 ✓

In general:
- A = (n-1) + S
- total_pairs = n*(n-1)/2
- B = total_pairs - A
- sum = 1*A + 2*B = A + 2*(total_pairs - A) = 2*total_pairs - A

So sum = 2*(n*(n-1)/2) - ((n-1) + S) = n*(n-1) - (n-1) - S = (n-1)*(n-2) - S

But this gives (4-1)*(4-2) - 3 = 6 - 3 = 3, not 6.

Where is the error?

I see! In this formula, I wrote:
sum = n*(n-1) - (n-1) - S

But n*(n-1) - (n-1) - S = (n-1)*(n-1) - S = (n-1)² - S

For example 1: (4-1)² - 3 = 9 - 3 = 6 ✓

And for example 2: n=4, S = ? 
Tree: 1-2-3-4
deg(1)=1, deg(2)=2, deg(3)=2, deg(4)=1
S = C(1,2) + C(2,2) + C(2,2) + C(1,2) = 0 + 1 + 1 + 0 = 2

Sum = (4-1)² - 2 = 9 - 2 = 7 ✓

Perfect!

The formula is: sum = (n-1)² - Σ C(deg(w), 2)

Where the sum is over all nodes w.

This is because:
- All pairs have distance at most 2
- The pairs that have distance 1 are all pairs that are either directly connected or share a common neighbor
- The pairs that have distance 2 are the rest
- But the total sum is (n-1)² - Σ C(deg(w), 2)

Wait, let me verify the logic:

sum = 2*total_pairs - A = 2*(n*(n-1)/2) - ((n-1) + S) = n*(n-1) - (n-1) - S = (n-1)*(n-1) - S = (n-1)² - S

Yes! The total sum of distances in the new graph is (n-1)² - Σ C(deg(w), 2)

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 1:
    print(0)
else:
    deg = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        deg[u] += 1
        deg[v] += 1
    
    total_sum = (n - 1) * (n - 1)
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        d = deg[i]
        total_sum -= d * (d - 1) // 2
    
    print(total_sum)
```

Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----

Output

7

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations) and n-1 edges (tunnels)

2. After adding these edges, the new graph will have a specific structure

**Example 1:** Star graph (1 connected to 2,3,4)
- Original: edges (1-2), (1-3), (1-4)
- Any two nodes that share a common neighbor: 2 and 3 share neighbor 1, 2 and 4 share neighbor 1, 3 and 4 share neighbor 1
- So we add edges (2-3), (2-4), (3-4)
- Now we have a complete graph K4 with all pairs connected directly

**Example 2:** Path graph (1-2-3-4)
- Original: edges (1-2), (2-3), (3-4)

- Sum: 1+1+1+1+1+2 = 7

## Solution Approach

The key insight is that in the new graph:
- All pairs of nodes that were at distance 1 or 2 in original tree now have distance 1
- All pairs of nodes that were at distance 3 or more in original tree now have distance equal to their original distance

## Algorithm

1. Build adjacency list from input
2. Calculate distance between every pair of nodes using BFS from each node (or use tree DP)
3. For each pair, if distance <= 2, add 1 to sum; if distance > 2, add distance to sum

But n can be up to 200,000, so O(n²) is too slow.

We need a better approach.

## Optimized Approach

We can calculate:
- Sum of distances in new graph = (number of pairs with distance <= 2) * 1 + (sum of distances for pairs with distance > 2)

For distance <= 2 pairs:
- Distance 1 pairs: all edges in tree → count = n-1
- Distance 2 pairs: pairs of nodes with exactly one common neighbor

For distance > 2 pairs: we need to sum distances in original tree for all pairs at distance > 2.

Actually, we can use the fact that the sum of distances in a tree has a known formula, and we can find the sum for pairs at distance 1,2 separately.

Let me implement using tree DP to count pairs at distance 1,2 and sum distances at distance > 2.

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
        edges.append((u, v))
    
    # Calculate distances between all pairs using BFS from each node
    # This is O(n^2) which might be too slow for n=200000
    # We need a better approach
    
    # Instead, let's think differently:
    # In new graph:
    # - All pairs with original distance 1: distance 1
    # - All pairs with original distance 2: distance 1  
    # - All pairs with original distance > 2: distance = original distance
    
    # So sum = (number of pairs with d=1) + (number of pairs with d=2) + (sum of distances for pairs with d>2)
    # = (n-1) + (number of pairs with d=2) + (sum of distances for pairs with d>2)
    
    # We need to count pairs with d=2 and sum distances for d>2
    
    # First, count pairs with distance exactly 2 in original tree
    # For each node as center, count pairs of its neighbors that are not directly connected
    # But they are connected only if they share the center
    
    pairs_with_dist_2 = 0
    total_distance_for_dist_gt_2 = 0
    
    # For each node, count pairs of its neighbors
    for u in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[u]
        k = len(neighbors)
        # Each pair of neighbors (v,w) forms a path of length 2
        # We need to be careful not to double count
        for i in range(k):
            for j in range(i + 1, k):
                v, w = neighbors[i], neighbors[j]
                # This pair (v,w) has distance 2 in original tree
                pairs_with_dist_2 += 1
    
    # Now calculate sum of distances for all pairs with distance > 2
    # We can use tree DP to calculate sum of distances between all pairs
    # Then subtract sum of distances for pairs with distance 1 and 2
    
    # Sum of distances in original tree
    total_original_sum = 0
    
    # We'll calculate using BFS from each node
    # For large n, this is O(n^2), but let's see if there's a tree DP approach
    
    # Since n can be up to 200000, O(n^2) won't work
    # Need to use tree DP to calculate sum of all pairwise distances
    
    # Alternative: use known tree distance formula
    # Sum of distances in tree = sum over edges of (size_of_component1 * size_of_component2)
    
    def get_sum_of_distances():
        # For tree, sum of distances = sum over each edge of (size of left subtree * size of right subtree)
        # where sizes are when removing the edge
        
        total = 0
        visited = [False] * (n + 1)
        
        def dfs(u, parent, size):
            size[u] = 1
            for v in adj[u]:
                if v != parent:
                    dfs(v, u, size)
                    size[u] += size[v]
                    total += size[v] * (n - size[v])
        
        size = [0] * (n + 1)
        dfs(1, -1, size)
        return total
    
    # But this gives sum of all distances, including those of distance 1 and 2
    # We want sum of distances for pairs with distance > 2
    
    # Let's calculate sum of distances for pairs with distance 1 and 2
    sum_dist_1_2 = 0
    
    # Distance 1 pairs: there are n-1 such pairs
    sum_dist_1_2 += n - 1
    
    # Distance 2 pairs: there are pairs_with_dist_2 such pairs, each contributing distance 2
    # (wait no! distance of such pairs is 2 in original tree, but in new graph it becomes 1)
    
    # Actually, in our final answer:
    # For each pair with d_original = 1: contributes 1 to sum
    # For each pair with d_original = 2: contributes 1 to sum  
    # For each pair with d_original = 3 or more: contributes d_original to sum
    
    # So total = (n-1) + pairs_with_dist_2 + (sum of d_original for all pairs with d_original > 2)
    
    # We have total_original_sum = sum of d_original for all pairs
    # total_original_sum = sum_{d=1} + sum_{d=2} + sum_{d>=3}
    # = sum_{d=1} * 1 + sum_{d=2} * 2 + sum_{d>=3} * d
    
    # Therefore, sum of d_original for d>=3 = total_original_sum - (n-1) - 2 * pairs_with_dist_2
    
    # So our answer becomes:
    # result = (n-1) + pairs_with_dist_2 + (total_original_sum - (n-1) - 2 * pairs_with_dist_2)
    #        = total_original_sum - pairs_with_dist_2
    
    total_original_sum = get_sum_of_distances()
    result = total_original_sum - pairs_with_dist_2
```

- So C = total_original_sum - (n-1) - 2 * pairs_with_dist_2

Also, A + B = total_pairs = C(n,2) = n*(n-1)/2

So B = n*(n-1)/2 - A = n*(n-1)/2 - (n-1) - pairs_with_dist_2

Therefore:

Since A = (n-1) + pairs_with_dist_2

new_sum = n*(n-1) - (n-1) - pairs_with_dist_2 = (n-1)*(n-2) - pairs_with_dist_2

```python
from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        return
        
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # For each node, count pairs of its neighbors (these have distance 2)
    pairs_with_dist_2 = 0
    for u in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[u]
        k = len(neighbors)
        # Each pair of neighbors has distance 2
        pairs_with_dist_2 += k * (k - 1) // 2
    
    # Calculate sum of pairwise distances in original tree using tree DP
    # Sum of distances = sum over edges of (size of left component * size of right component)
    total_original_sum = 0
    
    # We need to calculate size of subtrees when removing each edge
    # We'll do DFS from node 1
    size = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    def dfs(u, parent):
        size[u] = 1
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v != parent:
                dfs(v, u)
                size[u] += size[v]
                # When removing edge (u,v), components have sizes size[v] and (n - size[v])
                # Contribution to sum: size[v] * (n - size[v])
                nonlocal total_original_sum
                total_original_sum += size[v] * (n - size[v])
    
    dfs(1, -1)
    
    # Total pairs = n*(n-1)/2
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # For new graph:
    # All pairs with distance <= 2 have distance 1 in new graph
    # All pairs with distance > 2 have distance 2 in new graph
    
    # Number of pairs with distance <= 2 = (n-1) + pairs_with_dist_2
    pairs_with_dist_le_2 = (n - 1) + pairs_with_dist_2
    
    # Number of pairs with distance > 2 = total_pairs - pairs_with_dist_le_2
    pairs_with_dist_gt_2 = total_pairs - pairs_with_dist_le_2
    
    # Sum of new distances = 1 * pairs_with_dist_le_2 + 2 * pairs_with_dist_gt_2
    new_sum = pairs_with_dist_le_2 + 2 * pairs_with_dist_gt_2
    
    print(new_sum)

solve()
```

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        return
        
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # For each node, count pairs of its neighbors (these have distance 2)
    pairs_with_dist_2 = 0
    for u in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[u]
        k = len(neighbors)
        pairs_with_dist_2 += k * (k - 1) // 2
    
    # Calculate sum of pairwise distances in original tree using tree DP
    # Sum of distances = sum over edges of (size of left component * size of right component)
    total_original_sum = 0
    
    size = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    def dfs(u, parent):
        size[u] = 1
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v != parent:
                dfs(v, u)
                size[u] += size[v]
                total_original_sum += size[v] * (n - size[v])
    
    dfs(1, -1)
    
    # Total pairs = n*(n-1)/2
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # For new graph:
    # All pairs with distance <= 2 have distance 1 in new graph
    # All pairs with distance > 2 have distance 2 in new graph
    
    pairs_with_dist_le_2 = (n - 1) + pairs_with_dist_2
    
    pairs_with_dist_gt_2 = total_pairs - pairs_with_dist_le_2
```

from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        return
        
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # For each node, count pairs of its neighbors (these have distance 2)
    pairs_with_dist_2 = 0
    for u in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[u]
        k = len(neighbors)
        pairs_with_dist_2 += k * (k - 1) // 2
    
    # Calculate sum of pairwise distances in original tree using tree DP
    # Sum of distances = sum over edges of (size of left component * size of right component)
    total_original_sum = 0
    
    size = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    def dfs(u, parent):
        size[u] = 1
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v != parent:
                dfs(v, u)
                size[u] += size[v]
                total_original_sum += size[v] * (n - size[v])
    
    dfs(1, -1)
    
    # Total pairs = n*(n-1)/2
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # For new graph:
    # All pairs with distance <= 2 have distance 1 in new graph
    # All pairs with distance > 2 have distance 2 in new graph
    
    pairs_with_dist_le_2 = (n - 1) + pairs_with_dist_2
    
    pairs_with_dist_gt_2 = total_pairs - pairs_with_dist_le_2
    
    new_sum = pairs_with_dist_le_2 + 2 * pairs_with_dist_gt_2

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). However, his assistants have found a solution and they now draw an imaginary city on a paper sheet and suggest the mayor can propose its improvements.

Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

Input
4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$.
```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # For each node, get all its neighbors
    neighbors = {}
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors[i] = set(adj[i])
    
    # In the new graph, two nodes are connected if they share a common neighbor
    # We'll create a new adjacency list
    new_adj = defaultdict(set)
    
    # Add direct edges between nodes that share common neighbors
    for u in range(1, n + 1):
        for v in adj[u]:
            for w in adj[u]:
                if v != w:
                    # u and w share neighbor v? No
                    # We need to check if u and w share a common neighbor
                    # Actually, u and w share a common neighbor if there exists x such that x is adjacent to both u and w
                    pass
    
    # This approach is too slow. Let me think differently.
    
    # Actually, after adding edges, the new graph has a special structure.
    # For each node, its neighbors form a clique (because they all share the node as common neighbor).
    
    # So for any three nodes a,b,c where c is the common neighbor, a and b have direct edge.
    
    # In the new graph:
    # - All pairs that share a common neighbor have distance 1
    # - For other pairs, distance is 2 or more
    
    # But in a tree, pairs that don't share a common neighbor might still be at distance 2.
    
    # Let me calculate the distance between every pair:
    # For pair (i,j), if there exists k such that k is adjacent to both i and j, distance is 1
    # Otherwise, distance might be 2 or more.
    
    # Actually, in the new graph, the distance between two nodes i,j is:
    # 1 if they share a common neighbor
    # Otherwise, the distance is at least 2
    
    # But the minimum distance might be 2 even if they don't share a common neighbor?
    
    # For example, in a path 1-2-3-4, 1 and 4 don't share common neighbor, but distance might be 2 via path 1-2-3-4?
    
    # Actually, with edges added: 1-3, 2-4
    # So distance(1,4) = 2 (1-3-4 or 1-2-4)
    
    # So in general, after the edges are added, the distance between any two nodes is at most 2?
    
    # Yes! Because if two nodes don't share a common neighbor, they might be at distance 2 in the path.
    
    # Actually, no. In a star graph, all nodes share the center, so all pairs have distance 1.
    
    # The key insight: in the new graph, every pair of nodes has distance at most 2.
    
    # So the sum of distances is:
    # Count of pairs with distance 1 + 2 * (count of pairs with distance 2)
    
    # Pairs with distance 1: pairs that share a common neighbor
    # Pairs with distance 2: pairs that don't share a common neighbor
    
    # Count pairs that share a common neighbor:
    # For each node u, let d_u be the degree of u
    # The number of pairs of neighbors of u is C(d_u, 2)
    # So total pairs with distance 1 = sum over all u of C(deg(u), 2)
    
    # Total pairs = C(n, 2)
    # So pairs with distance 2 = C(n, 2) - sum over u of C(deg(u), 2)
    
    # Then sum of distances = 1 * (pairs with distance 1) + 2 * (pairs with distance 2)
    # = (sum C(deg(u), 2)) + 2 * (C(n,2) - sum C(deg(u), 2))
    # = 2 * C(n,2) - sum C(deg(u), 2)
    
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    dist1_count = 0
    for u in range(1, n + 1):
        deg = len(adj[u])
        dist1_count += deg * (deg - 1) // 2
    
    dist2_count = total_pairs - dist1_count
    
    result = dist1_count * 1 + dist2_count * 2
    print(result)
```

Therefore, we can compute:
- Sum of distances = (number of pairs with distance 1) + 2 * (number of pairs with distance 2)

- Pairs with distance 1: pairs that are directly connected in the new graph
- Pairs with distance 2: pairs that are not directly connected but are at distance 2 in the new graph

The pairs with distance 1 are exactly the pairs that share a common neighbor in the original tree.

The pairs with distance 2 are the pairs that don't share a common neighbor, but in the new graph, they might still have a path of length 2.

In the new graph, a pair (u,v) has distance 2 if there is no direct edge between them and they are not adjacent via a path of length 2.

I think yes, because the structure forces paths to be at most 2 steps apart.

```python
    print(1)

    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]

        u, v = map(int, input().split())

        adj[v].append(u)

    # For each node, count pairs of its neighbors
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). However, his assistants have found a solution and they now draw an imaginary city on a paper sheet and suggest the mayor can propose its improvements.

Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

Input
4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7



-----Note-----

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$. I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations) and n-1 edges (tunnels)
2. We add new tunnels between any two stations u and v that share a common neighbor w (meaning u-w and w-v exist in the original tree)
3. We need to find the sum of distances between all pairs of stations in the new graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, any two nodes have a unique path between them
2. When we add edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, we're essentially creating a graph where each "star" center (node with multiple neighbors) creates a complete subgraph among its neighbors
3. The key observation is that after adding these edges, the graph becomes a "clustered" graph where each component is a complete graph (clique) centered at each node that has degree ≥ 2

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1**: Complete star with center 1 connected to 2,3,4
- Original: paths are 1-2, 1-3, 1-4
- After adding edges: all pairs have direct connections
- Distances: (1,2)=1, (1,3)=1, (1,4)=1, (2,3)=1, (2,4)=1, (3,4)=1
- Sum = 6

**Example 2**: Linear chain 1-2-3-4
- Original edges: 1-2, 2-3, 3-4
- Nodes sharing common neighbors:
  - Node 2: neighbors 1,3 → adds edge (1,3)
  - Node 3: neighbors 2,4 → adds edge (2,4)
- New edges: (1,3), (2,4)
- Final structure: 
  - 1 connected to 2,3
  - 2 connected to 1,3,4
  - 3 connected to 1,2,4
  - 4 connected to 2,3
- Distance(1,4): path 1-2-4 or 1-3-4, so distance = 2
- All other distances are 1
- Sum = 1+1+1+1+1+2 = 7

## Solution Strategy

1. For each node, identify its neighbors
2. For each node with degree ≥ 2, all pairs of its neighbors will be directly connected
3. Calculate the distance between each pair in the new graph

The new graph has the property that:
- For each node w, all neighbors of w form a complete subgraph (they are all directly connected)
- The distance between two nodes u and v:
  - If u and v are neighbors of the same node w, distance = 1
  - Otherwise, we need to consider the paths through common neighbors

Actually, let me reconsider. In the new graph:
- For each node w, all neighbors of w are directly connected to each other
- So the graph becomes a union of cliques, where each clique is formed by the neighbors of some node w

The distance between two nodes u and v:
- If u and v are in the same clique (they share a common neighbor), distance = 1
- Otherwise, the distance is at least 2, and we need to find the minimum path

But actually, in a tree with additional edges between neighbors of common nodes, the distance between any two nodes u and v is at most 2:
- If u and v share a common neighbor, distance = 1
- Otherwise, they must be at distance 2 (through some path)

Wait, let me think again. If u and v don't share a common neighbor, what's the distance?

Actually, in the new graph, the distance is:
- If u and v have a common neighbor, distance = 1
- Otherwise, distance = 2 (since if they don't share a common neighbor, they might still have a path of length 2 through some intermediate node)

But is that always true? Let me verify with the chain example:
- 1 and 4 don't share a common neighbor in the original tree
- In new graph, distance between 1 and 4 is 2 (path 1-2-4 or 1-3-4)

So the distance between any two nodes is:
- 1 if they share a common neighbor in the original tree
- 2 otherwise

This makes sense because:
- If they share a common neighbor w, they're directly connected in the new graph
- If they don't share a common neighbor, the shortest path will go through one of their neighbors (distance 2)

## Algorithm

1. Build adjacency list from input
2. For each pair of nodes, determine if they share a common neighbor
3. If they share a common neighbor, distance = 1
4. Otherwise, distance = 2
5. Sum all distances

However, this would be O(n²) which might be too slow for n=200,000.

We need a more efficient approach:

We can count:
- Number of pairs that share a common neighbor (distance = 1)
- Number of pairs that don't share a common neighbor (distance = 2)
- Total sum = (number of pairs with distance 1) * 1 + (number of pairs with distance 2) * 2

Pairs with distance 1: For each node w, the number of pairs among its neighbors is C(degree(w), 2)

Total pairs: C(n, 2) = n*(n-1)/2

Pairs with distance 2: C(n, 2) - sum(C(degree(w), 2) for all w)

Total sum = sum(C(degree(w), 2)) + 2 * (C(n, 2) - sum(C(degree(w), 2)))

Simplified: total_sum = 2 * C(n, 2) - sum(C(degree(w), 2))

Let me verify with examples:

**Example 1**: n=4, degrees: 1:3, 2:1, 3:1, 4:1
- C(3,2) = 3
- C(4,2) = 6
- Sum = 2*6 - 3 = 12 - 3 = 9 ❌

Wait, this doesn't match output 6.

Let me reconsider.

In example 1, after adding edges, all pairs have distance 1. So sum should be 6.

My earlier analysis was wrong. Let me fix it.

In example 1: 
- Node 1 has degree 3, so neighbors {2,3,4} form a complete subgraph
- Node 2 has degree 1, so no new edges

- For each pair that is directly connected (original or new), distance = 1
- For each pair that is not directly connected and has no common neighbor, distance = 2

Number of pairs with distance 1 = number of edges in the new graph
Number of pairs with distance 2 = C(n,2) - number of edges in new graph

Sum = (edges) * 1 + (C(n,2) - edges) * 2 = 2* C(n,2) - edges

So we need to count the number of edges in the new graph.

New edges are between u and v such that:
- u and v are not adjacent in original tree
- u and v share a common neighbor

Number of new edges = sum over all nodes w of (number of pairs of neighbors of w that are not adjacent in original tree)

But we need to be careful: if two nodes are already adjacent in the original tree, we don't add an edge.

For a node w with degree d, let its neighbors be {v1, v2, ..., vd}
Number of pairs among them: C(d,2)
Number of pairs that are adjacent in original tree: for each edge (vi, vj) in the original tree, if it exists between two neighbors of w, then it's an edge we don't add.

In a tree, can two neighbors of w be adjacent in the original tree?

Only if they are connected through w. In a tree, if vi and vj are both neighbors of w, then the path from vi to vj goes through w, so they are not directly adjacent unless the tree has a direct edge.

In a tree, if vi and vj are both adjacent to w, then the path from vi to vj is vi-w-vj, so there is no direct edge between vi and vj.

Therefore, in the original tree, no two neighbors of w are directly adjacent.

So for each node w, all C(d(w),2) pairs of its neighbors are not directly connected and share common neighbor w.

Therefore, number of new edges = sum over all w of C(d(w),2)

Then total sum of distances = 2 * C(n,2) - sum over w of C(d(w),2)

For example 1:
- n=4, C(4,2) = 6
- degrees: node 1 has degree 3, others have degree 1

- 1 if either they were originally adjacent, or they share a common neighbor
- 2 otherwise

- original edges: in a tree, there are n-1 edges

But (2,3) is directly connected, so distance 1.

So pairs with distance 1: (1,2), (1,3), (2,3), (2,4), (3,4) — 5 pairs.

The condition for two nodes to have a common neighbor is that there exists w such that w is adjacent to both.

For (2,3): is there w such that w is adjacent to 2 and to 3?
- w adjacent to 2: 1,3
- w adjacent to 3: 2,4
- Common w? Only if w is adjacent to both 2 and 3.
- w=2: adjacent to 1,3 → adjacent to 3, not to 2
- w=3: adjacent to 2,4 → adjacent to 2, not to 3
- So no common neighbor.

So (2,3) has no common neighbor, but they are directly connected, so distance 1.

Therefore, distance is 1 if:
- the two nodes are directly connected in the original tree, or
- they have a common neighbor in the original tree

So total pairs with distance 1 = (n-1) + sum over w of C(d(w),2)

For example 1:
- n-1 = 3
- sum_C = C(3,2) = 3
- total = 3 + 3 = 6
- sum = 6*1 + 0*2 = 6 ✓

For example 2:
- n-1 = 3
- sum_C = C(2,2) for w=2 = 1, C(2,2) for w=3 = 1, others 0, so sum_C = 2
- total = 3 + 2 = 5
- pairs with distance 2 = 6 - 5 = 1
- sum = 5*1 + 1*2 = 7 ✓

Perfect!

Therefore, the solution is:

1. Read the tree
2. Compute degrees of each node
3. Compute sum of C(deg(w), 2) for all w
4. total pairs with distance 1 = (n-1) + sum_C
5. pairs with distance 2 = C(n,2) - (n-1) - sum_C
6. total sum = (n-1 + sum_C) * 1 + (C(n,2) - (n-1) - sum_C) * 2

Simplify:
= (n-1 + sum_C) + 2*(n*(n-1)/2 - (n-1) - sum_C)
= (n-1 + sum_C) + (n*(n-1) - 2*(n-1) - 2*sum_C)
= (n-1 + sum_C) + (n-1)(n-2) - 2*sum_C
= (n-1) + sum_C + (n-1)(n-2) - 2*sum_C
= (n-1)(1 + n-2) - sum_C
= (n-1)(n-1) - sum_C
= (n-1)² - sum_C

So total sum = (n-1)² - sum_{w} C(deg(w), 2)

Let me verify:

Example 1: n=4, (4-1)² = 9, sum_C = 3, 9-3 = 6 ✓

Example 2: n=4, (4-1)² = 9, sum_C = 2, 9-2 = 7 ✓

Perfect!

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 1:
    print(0)
else:
    # Read edges and compute degrees
    deg = [0] * (n + 1)
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        deg[u] += 1
        deg[v] += 1
    
    # Compute sum of C(deg(w), 2) for all nodes w
    sum_comb = 0
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        d = deg[i]
        if d >= 2:
            sum_comb += d * (d - 1) // 2
    
    # Total sum of pairwise distances = (n-1)^2 - sum_comb
    result = (n - 1) * (n - 1) - sum_comb
    print(result)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). However, his assistants have found a solution and they now draw an imaginary city on a paper sheet and suggest the mayor can propose its improvements.

Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

Input
4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7



-----Note-----

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$. Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations)
2. We add new edges between any two stations that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of pairwise distances in the resulting graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, any two nodes that share a common neighbor will have a direct edge added
2. After adding these edges, we need to find the sum of distances between all pairs of nodes
3. The resulting graph will have a special structure - it's a "clustered" tree

## Analysis of the Examples

**Example 1**: Star graph (center 1 connected to 2,3,4)
- Original: 1-2, 1-3, 1-4
- After adding edges: All pairs share common neighbor 1, so we add edges (2,3), (2,4), (3,4)
- Result: Complete graph K4 with 4 nodes
- Distance between any two nodes = 1
- Sum of distances = 4×3/2 × 1 = 6 ✓

**Example 2**: Path graph (1-2-3-4)
- Original: 1-2, 2-3, 3-4
- Common neighbors:
  - 1 and 3: common neighbor 2
  - 1 and 4: common neighbor 3? No, 1 has neighbor 2, 4 has neighbor 3
  - 2 and 4: common neighbor 3
  - 2 and 3: already connected
  - 1 and 2: already connected
  - 3 and 4: already connected
- So we add edges (1,3) and (2,4)
- New graph: 1-2-3-4 with additional 1-3 and 2-4
- Distances:
  - (1,2): 1
  - (1,3): 1  
  - (1,4): 2 (path 1-2-4 or 1-3-4)
  - (2,3): 1
  - (2,4): 1
  - (3,4): 1
- Sum = 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7 ✓

## Solution Approach

The key insight is that after adding all edges between nodes with a common neighbor, the resulting graph has a special property. We need to compute the sum of distances between all pairs.

Actually, let me think more systematically:

In the final graph, two nodes are directly connected if:
1. They were already connected in the original tree, OR
2. They share a common neighbor in the original tree

So the final graph is the union of the original tree and all edges between nodes that share a common neighbor.

To compute the sum of distances, I can:
1. For each pair of nodes, compute their distance in the final graph
2. Sum all distances

However, with n up to 200,000, I can't check all pairs naively.

Alternative approach:
- In the final graph, many pairs will have distance 1
- For each node, I can compute how many pairs have distance 1, and how many have distance > 1

Wait, let me reconsider. After adding edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, the resulting graph will have:
- All nodes that are at distance ≤ 2 from a common neighbor become mutually connected

Actually, the final graph consists of connected components that are cliques. Two nodes are in the same clique if they can be reached through a path of length at most 2 from a common node.

I think a better approach is:
1. Find all pairs that now have direct edges
2. The final distance between two nodes is the minimum of:
   - The original distance (if direct edge exists)
   - The distance through a common neighbor (distance = 2)

But since we add edges between any two nodes that share a common neighbor, the resulting graph will have the property that any two nodes that are at distance ≤ 2 in the original tree will have a path of length 1 or 2.

Actually, let's implement a solution that computes the final distances efficiently.

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list for original tree
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Find all pairs that share a common neighbor and add direct edges
    # First, identify all pairs that will have direct connections
    
    # For each possible common neighbor, add edges between all its neighbors
    edges = set()
    for node in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[node]
        # Add edges between every pair of neighbors of this node
        for i in range(len(neighbors)):
            for j in range(i + 1, len(neighbors)):
                u, v = neighbors[i], neighbors[j]
                edges.add((min(u, v), max(u, v)))
    
    # Convert edges to adjacency list for final graph
    final_adj = defaultdict(list)
    for u, v in edges:
        final_adj[u].append(v)
        final_adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Add original edges as well (they might not be in the new edges set)
    for u in range(1, n + 1):
        for v in adj[u]:
            if (u, v) not in edges and (v, u) not in edges:
                final_adj[u].append(v)
                final_adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Now compute sum of distances between all pairs
    total_sum = 0
    
    # For each pair, compute shortest distance in final graph
    # We'll use BFS from each node
    for start in range(1, n + 1):
        dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
        queue = deque([start])
        dist[start] = 0
        
        while queue:
            node = queue.popleft()
            for neighbor in final_adj[node]:
                if dist[neighbor] == -1:
                    dist[neighbor] = dist[node] + 1
                    queue.append(neighbor)
        
        # Add all distances from start to other nodes
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            if i != start:
                total_sum += dist[i]
    
    print(total_sum)

solve()
```

Wait, this approach might be too slow for n = 200,000 because computing BFS for each node would be O(n²).

Let me reconsider. There's likely a more efficient mathematical approach.
