from collections import defaultdict, deque

n = int(input())

if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list for original tree
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    edges_list = []
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
        edges_list.append((u, v))
    
    # Find all pairs that share a common neighbor
    new_edges = set()
    
    for node in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[node]
        # Add edges between every pair of neighbors of this node
        for i in range(len(neighbors)):
            for j in range(i + 1, len(neighbors)):
                u, v = neighbors[i], neighbors[j]
                new_edges.add((min(u, v), max(u, v)))
    
    # Build final adjacency list
    final_adj = defaultdict(list)
    
    # Add original edges
    for u, v in edges_list:
        final_adj[u].append(v)
        final_adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Add new edges
    for u, v in new_edges:
        final_adj[u].append(v)
        final_adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Compute sum of distances using BFS from each node
    total_sum = 0
    
    for start in range(1, n + 1):
        dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
        queue = deque([start])
        dist[start] = 0
        
        while queue:
            node = queue.popleft()
            for neighbor in final_adj[node]:
                if dist[neighbor] == -1:
                    dist[neighbor] = dist[node] + 1
                    queue.append(neighbor)
        
        # Add distances from start to all other nodes
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            if i != start:
                total_sum += dist[i]
    
    print(total_sum)

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$. ## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations)
2. We add new edges between any two stations that share a common neighbor in the original tree

2. The new graph will have edges between:
   - All original edges (from the tree)
   - All pairs of nodes that share a common neighbor

3. Let me analyze when two nodes share a common neighbor:
   - If two nodes u and v have a common neighbor w, then u-w and w-v exist in the original tree
   - After adding edges, u and v will be directly connected

4. The resulting graph structure:
   - For a node w with degree d, all nodes adjacent to w will be directly connected to each other
   - So we get a clique (complete subgraph) on all neighbors of w

## Step-by-step Analysis

Let's think about what happens to distances:

1. **Original tree distances**: In a tree, distance between two nodes is the sum of edges on the unique path.

2. **After adding edges**: 
   - Any two nodes in the same "neighbor group" of a common node will have distance 1
   - For nodes not directly connected, we need to find the shortest path

3. **Key observation**: 
   - After adding all edges between nodes sharing a common neighbor, the resulting graph becomes much more connected
   - For any two nodes u and v, if they are in the same "clique" (same neighbor group), distance = 1
   - If they are in different neighbor groups, we need to find the minimum path

4. **Final structure**: The new graph will have:
   - Each "hub" node creates a clique of its neighbors
   - Within each clique, distance between any two nodes = 1
   - Between different cliques, the distance depends on the structure

## Algorithm

1. Build the original tree
2. For each node, identify all its neighbors

## Key Insight: Distance Analysis

In the new graph:
- Any two nodes that are in the same "neighbor group" of a common node have distance 1
- Any two nodes that don't share a common neighbor might have distance 2 if they're connected through a path of length 2

- 1 if they share a common neighbor (or are directly connected through an original edge)

- Otherwise 1

- Original edges remain
- We add edges between nodes that share a common neighbor
- In the resulting graph, two nodes have distance 1 if they are in the same "clique" (same neighbor group of some node)

So the distance between any two nodes is either 1 or 2.

Two nodes have distance 2 if and only if they do not share a common neighbor AND are not adjacent in the original tree.

Actually, no. If they don't share a common neighbor but are on a path, they might still have distance 2.

Let me think differently:

After adding all edges between nodes sharing a common neighbor, the distance between any two nodes u and v is:
- 1 if there exists a node w such that both u and v are neighbors of w (they share a common neighbor)
- 2 if no such w exists and they are connected through a path of length 2
- Otherwise 1

But in a tree, any two nodes have a unique path. After adding these edges, the distance will be at most 2.

The distance between u and v is:
- 1 if they share a common neighbor
- 2 otherwise

Wait, not quite. Consider two nodes that don't share a common neighbor but are adjacent in the original tree - they still have distance 1.

So distance(u,v) = 1 if u and v are adjacent in the original tree OR they share a common neighbor.
Otherwise, distance(u,v) = 2.

But is that correct? In example 2:
- (1,2): original edge → distance 1 ✓
- (2,3): original edge → distance 1 ✓  
- (3,4): original edge → distance 1 ✓
- (1,3): share neighbor 2 → distance 1 ✓
- (2,4): share neighbor 3 → distance 1 ✓
- (1,4): no direct edge, no common neighbor → distance 2 ✓

So distance(u,v) = 1 if u and v are adjacent in the original tree OR they share a common neighbor, else 2.

Therefore, sum of distances = (# of pairs with distance 1) + 2*(# of pairs with distance 2)

Total pairs = n*(n-1)/2

# of pairs with distance 1 = total pairs - # of pairs with distance 2

Let me define:
- Let D = set of pairs (u,v) such that u and v are not adjacent in original tree AND don't share a common neighbor
- Then sum of distances = (total_pairs - |D|) * 1 + |D| * 2 = total_pairs + |D|

So answer = n*(n-1)/2 + |D|

Now I need to compute |D| - the number of pairs of nodes that are not adjacent in original tree AND don't share a common neighbor.

Two nodes u,v don't share a common neighbor if for every common neighbor w of u and v, w must be a neighbor of both u and v, but there's no such w.

Actually, u and v don't share a common neighbor if there's no node w such that w is adjacent to both u and v.

So |D| = number of unordered pairs {u,v} such that:
1. u and v are not adjacent in original tree
2. There is no node w such that w is adjacent to both u and v

In other words, u and v are "independent" in the sense that they don't "see" each other through any common neighbor.

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    edges = []
    
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
        edges.append((u, v))
    
    # Count pairs that have distance 2
    # Two nodes (u,v) have distance 2 if:
    # 1. They are not adjacent in original tree
    # 2. They do not share a common neighbor
    
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    dist_2_pairs = 0
    
    # For each pair of nodes, check if they don't share a common neighbor
    # But this is O(n^3) which is too slow for n=200000
    
    # Alternative approach: 
    # Count pairs that DO share a common neighbor, then subtract from total pairs
    
    # Total pairs that share a common neighbor
    # For each node w, the number of pairs among its neighbors is C(deg(w), 2)
    # But we need to be careful: if a pair (u,v) is counted in multiple w's, we need to avoid double counting?
    
    # Actually, if two nodes u,v share a common neighbor w, they have exactly one such w on the path (the LCA)?
    # No, they could share multiple common neighbors in a tree? Actually in a tree, two nodes have at most one common neighbor that can be on the path, but they might have multiple common neighbors.
    
    # In fact, in a tree, two nodes can share multiple common neighbors. For example, if we have w connected to u and v and also connected to x and y, then u and v share w, u and x share w, etc.
    
    # But the pair (u,v) will be counted in all w that are common neighbors of u and v.
    # However, for distance calculation, we only care whether they share at least one common neighbor.
    
    # So pairs with distance 1 = pairs that are adjacent in original tree OR share at least one common neighbor
    
    # So pairs with distance 2 = pairs that are not adjacent in original tree AND don't share any common neighbor
    
    # We can compute:
    # pairs_with_distance_1 = 0
    # For each original edge: count 1
    # For each node w: all pairs of neighbors of w - but if they are already counted as adjacent, we don't double count?
    
    # Actually, if two nodes are adjacent in original tree, we still need to check if they share a common neighbor, but their distance is already 1.
    
    # So total distance sum = total_pairs + (number of pairs that have distance 2)
    
    # We need to count pairs (u,v) such that:
    # 1. (u,v) is not an edge in original tree
    # 2. u and v do not share any common neighbor
    
    # How to count this efficiently? Use complementary counting.
    
    # Let S = total pairs = n*(n-1)//2
    # Let A = pairs that are adjacent in original tree
    # Let B = pairs that share at least one common neighbor
    # Then pairs with distance 1 = A + B - A∩B (where A∩B is pairs that are adjacent and share a common neighbor)
    
    # But actually, pairs with distance 1 = A ∪ B = A + |B - A|
    
    # The pairs that don't share a common neighbor and are not adjacent is S - (A + |B - A|) = S - A - |B - A|
    
    # So answer = S + (S - A - |B - A|) = 2S - A - |B - A|
    
    # Actually, let me redefine:
    # Let P = pairs with distance 1 = pairs that are adjacent or share a common neighbor
    # Let Q = pairs with distance 2 = pairs that are not adjacent and don't share a common neighbor
    # Then answer = P*1 + Q*2 = P + Q = P + (S - P) = S + Q
    # So answer = S + Q = S + (number of pairs with distance 2)
    
    # We need to compute Q = number of pairs (u,v) such that:
    # - (u,v) is not an edge in original tree
    # - u and v do not share a common neighbor
    
    # First compute S
    S = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Count how many pairs (u,v) are adjacent in original tree
    A = n - 1  # exactly n-1 edges
    
    # Count how many pairs (u,v) share at least one common neighbor
    # For each node w, the number of unordered pairs among its neighbors is C(deg(w), 2)
    # But this counts pairs that share a common neighbor, possibly multiple times if they share multiple common neighbors.
    
    # However, for our purpose, we only need to know if they share at least one common neighbor.
    # But the pair (u,v) might be counted in multiple w's.
    
    # Actually, for distance 1, we want to know if they share at least one common neighbor.
    # So we can use inclusion-exclusion, but it's complex in trees.
    
    # Alternative: In a tree, two nodes share a common neighbor if and only if there exists a node w such that w is adjacent to both.
    # And this happens precisely when u and v are not on a simple path that doesn't pass through w.
    
    # Actually, we can compute the total number of pairs that share at least one common neighbor using union of the cliques.
    
    # But since we only care about whether they share at least one common neighbor, we can use:
    # total_pairs_with_common_neighbor = sum over w of C(deg(w), 2) - duplicates
    
    # However, in general trees, pairs can share multiple common neighbors, so the sum over w of C(deg(w), 2) counts each pair multiple times.
    
    # We need the number of distinct pairs that share at least one common neighbor.
    
    # Actually, I don't think I need the exact count of pairs with common neighbor. I only need the count of pairs that don't share a common neighbor and aren't adjacent.
    
    # Let's compute total pairs that share at least one common neighbor using a set or by checking each potential pair.
    # But n is large.
    
    # Alternate insight: In the resulting graph, the distance between any two nodes is at most 2.
    # And in a tree transformed this way, we can prove that distance is 1 if they are adjacent or share a common neighbor, else 2.
    # So answer = sum_{pairs} distance = sum_{pairs} (1 if adjacent or share common neighbor, else 2)
    # = (number of pairs that are adjacent or share common neighbor) + (number of pairs that are neither)
    # = (adjacent_pairs + pairs_with_common_neighbor) - (adjacent_pairs_and_share_common_neighbor) + (pairs_with_distance_2)
    # = (adjacent_pairs + pairs_with_common_neighbor) + (pairs_with_distance_2)
    
    # Actually, the answer is = sum_{pairs} distance = 1 * (adjacent_pairs + pairs_with_common_neighbor) + 2 * (pairs_with_distance_2)
    # = adjacent_pairs + pairs_with_common_neighbor + 2 * (pairs_with_distance_2)
    # = adjacent_pairs + pairs_with_common_neighbor + 2 * (total_pairs - adjacent_pairs - pairs_with_common_neighbor)
    # = adjacent_pairs + pairs_with_common_neighbor + 2*total_pairs - 2*adjacent_pairs - 2*pairs_with_common_neighbor
    # = 2*total_pairs - adjacent_pairs - pairs_with_common_neighbor
    
    # Therefore: answer = 2*S - (n-1) - total_pairs_with_common_neighbor
    
    # So I need to compute total_pairs_with_common_neighbor = sum over all pairs (u,v) that share at least one common neighbor
    
    # But again, how to compute this efficiently?
    
    # Total_pairs_with_common_neighbor = sum over all nodes w of C(deg(w), 2) minus the overcounts.
    # In a tree, can a pair (u,v) share more than one common neighbor? Yes.
    
    # Actually, in a tree, if two nodes u and v share multiple common neighbors, the structure is special.
    # For example, if we have w1, w2 both connected to u and v, then u and v would have two common neighbors.
    
    # But in a tree, if u and v have two common neighbors w1 and w2, then w1 and w2 must be connected, and the path from u to v goes through both w1 and w2, which creates a cycle in the tree - contradiction.
    
    # Therefore, in a tree, any two distinct nodes can share at most one common neighbor.
    
    # This is a key property! In a tree, if two nodes have two common neighbors, then we would have a cycle.
    
    # So each pair of nodes shares at most one common neighbor.
    
    # Therefore, the total number of pairs that share at least one common neighbor is exactly sum over all nodes w of C(deg(w), 2).
    
    total_common_neighbor_pairs = 0
    for w in range(1, n+1):
        deg_w = len(adj[w])
        if deg_w >= 2:
            total_common_neighbor_pairs += deg_w * (deg_w - 1) // 2
    
    # Now, answer = 2*S - (n-1) - total_common_neighbor_pairs
    # But wait, is this correct?
    # answer = sum of distances = 2*S - (adjacent_pairs) - (pairs that share common neighbor)
    
    # adjacent_pairs = n-1
    # pairs that share common neighbor = total_common_neighbor_pairs
    
    result = 2 * S - (n - 1) - total_common_neighbor_pairs
    
    print(result)

solve()
```

**Example 1**: n=4, star graph
- S = 4*3//2 = 6
- adjacent_pairs = 3
- For node 1: deg=3, C(3,2)=3
- For nodes 2,3,4: deg=1, C(1,0)=0
- total_common_neighbor_pairs = 3
- result = 2*6 - 3 - 3 = 12 - 3 - 3 = 6 ✓

**Example 2**: n=4, path 1-2-3-4
- S = 6
- adjacent_pairs = 3
- deg: node1:1, node2:2, node3:2, node4:1
- C(1,2)=0, C(2,2)=1, C(2,2)=1, C(1,2)=0
- total_common_neighbor_pairs = 0+1+1+0 = 2
- result = 12 - 3 - 2 = 7 ✓

Perfect! The solution works.

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Total pairs of stations
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Count pairs that share at least one common neighbor
    # In a tree, any two nodes share at most one common neighbor (no cycles)
    common_neighbor_pairs = 0
    for w in range(1, n+1):
        deg_w = len(adj[w])
        if deg_w >= 2:
            common_neighbor_pairs += deg_w * (deg_w - 1) // 2
    
    # The answer is: 2*total_pairs - (n-1) - common_neighbor_pairs
    result = 2 * total_pairs - (n - 1) - common_neighbor_pairs
    print(result)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). However, his assistants have found a solution and they now draw an imaginary city on a paper sheet and suggest the mayor can propose its improvements.

Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

Input
4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7



-----Note-----

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$. I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations)
2. We add new edges between any two nodes that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of distances between all pairs of stations in the new graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, any two nodes that share a common neighbor will have a direct edge added
2. After adding these edges, the new graph will have certain properties:
   - If two nodes share a common neighbor, they become directly connected
   - This means that for any node w, all its neighbors become mutually connected
   - The resulting graph will have certain diameter constraints

Let me analyze the examples:

**Example 1:** Tree with edges (1,2), (1,3), (1,4)
- Node 1 has neighbors 2,3,4
- Since 2,3,4 all share neighbor 1, we add edges (2,3), (2,4), (3,4)
- Now all pairs are directly connected
- Sum of distances = 1+1+1+1+1+1 = 6

**Example 2:** Tree with edges (1,2), (2,3), (3,4)
- Node 2 has neighbors 1,3
- Node 3 has neighbors 2,4
- So we add edge (1,4) because both 1 and 4 have neighbor 2 (through 3) - actually, 1 and 4 don't share a direct common neighbor
- Let me reconsider: 
  - 1 and 3 share neighbor 2
  - 2 and 4 share neighbor 3  
  - 1 and 4 don't share a common neighbor
- So we add edge (1,3) and (2,4)
- The final edges are: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (1,3), (2,4)
- Distance(1,4) = 2 (path 1-3-4 or 1-2-4)
- Other distances are 1
- Sum = 1+1+1+1+1+2 = 7

## Pattern Recognition

After adding edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, what happens?

For any node w, all its neighbors become directly connected to each other (because they all share w as a common neighbor).

So for each node w with degree d_w, we add edges among all pairs of its neighbors. This means in the new graph:
- The subgraph induced by the neighbors of w becomes a complete graph
- This implies that any two nodes that are both neighbors of w can reach each other in distance 1

## Algorithm

1. Build the original tree
2. For each node w, find all its neighbors
3. In the new graph, all neighbors of w are directly connected
4. The distances between nodes:
   - If two nodes are adjacent in original tree, distance = 1
   - If two nodes share a common neighbor, distance = 1
   - Otherwise, we need to find the shortest path

Actually, let me think differently. After adding all edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, the resulting graph has a special structure:

For any two nodes u and v:
- If they share a common neighbor w, then distance(u,v) = 1
- If not, then the distance might be 2 or more

But looking at the examples, I notice that in both cases, the sum of distances is simple.

Alternative approach: The new graph contains all edges from the original tree plus all edges between nodes that share a common neighbor. This means:

1. For every node w, the neighbors of w form a clique (complete subgraph)
2. Two nodes u and v have distance at most 2 in the new graph

I think the key insight is that in the new graph, the diameter is at most 2. 

For the sum of distances:
- If u and v are adjacent in the original tree, distance = 1
- If u and v are not adjacent but share a common neighbor, distance = 1
- If u and v don't share a common neighbor and are not adjacent, distance = 2

- Sum of distances = (number of edges in original tree) * 1 + (number of non-adjacent pairs that share a common neighbor) * 1 + (number of non-adjacent pairs that don't share a common neighbor) * 2

But in a tree, two nodes share a common neighbor if and only if they are at distance 2 in the original tree. 

Actually, let me simplify:

2. Count all pairs that have distance 2

- All original edges

So the formula is:
- Distance(u,v) = 1 if u and v share a common neighbor (in original tree)
- Distance(u,v) = 2 otherwise

But what if u=v? No, we're talking about distinct pairs.

So total sum = (# of pairs that share a common neighbor) + 2 * (# of pairs that don't share a common neighbor)

Total pairs = n*(n-1)/2

So sum = (pairs with common neighbor) + 2 * (total pairs - pairs with common neighbor)
sum = (pairs with common neighbor) + 2 * (total pairs) - 2 * (pairs with common neighbor)
sum = 2 * (total pairs) - (pairs with common neighbor)

So I need to count the number of unordered pairs {u,v} such that u and v share a common neighbor.

For each node w, if w has degree d, then the number of pairs among its neighbors is C(d,2) = d*(d-1)/2.

But each pair {u,v} that shares a common neighbor is counted exactly once (since in a tree, two nodes can't share two different common neighbors? Actually, they might - but in a tree, if two nodes share more than one common neighbor, that would create a cycle, which is impossible).

Actually, in a tree, two nodes can share at most one common neighbor. So each pair {u,v} that shares a common neighbor is counted exactly once.

So total pairs with common neighbor = sum over all nodes w of C(deg(w), 2)

Total pairs = n*(n-1)/2

Therefore, answer = 2 * (n*(n-1)/2) - sum_over_w [deg(w)*(deg(w)-1)/2]
answer = n*(n-1) - sum_over_w [deg(w)*(deg(w)-1)/2]

Take u=1,v=2. They are adjacent, so distance=1.

So I need to count total pairs with distance 1 = adjacent pairs + pairs sharing common neighbor that are not adjacent.

But adjacent pairs are already included.

Actually, I should count:
- All pairs {u,v} such that there exists w where both u,w and v,w are edges in the tree.

This includes adjacent pairs (when w is one of the endpoints) and non-adjacent pairs that share a common neighbor.

For each node w, the number of pairs of its neighbors is C(deg(w), 2).

This counts every pair {u,v} that share a common neighbor w.

And since in a tree, two nodes can't share two common neighbors, each pair is counted exactly once.

So total pairs with distance 1 = sum_over_w [C(deg(w), 2)]

Total pairs = n*(n-1)/2

Total sum of distances = 1 * (pairs with distance 1) + 2 * (pairs with distance > 1)
= sum_over_w [C(deg(w), 2)] + 2 * (total pairs - sum_over_w [C(deg(w), 2)])
= 2 * total_pairs - sum_over_w [C(deg(w), 2)]

Sum = 2*6 - 3 = 12-3 = 9 ❌ (expected 6)

I think I'm missing something. In example 1, after adding all edges, all pairs have distance 1. So sum should be 6.

In my formula, I'm getting 9. 

The issue is that in example 1, when I add edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, I'm adding edges among all neighbors of each node.

For node 1 with neighbors 2,3,4: I add edges (2,3), (2,4), (3,4)
For other nodes: deg=1, so no additional edges.

So the graph has edges: (1,2), (1,3), (1,4), (2,3), (2,4), (3,4)
All pairs have distance 1. Number of pairs = 6. Sum = 6.

So the distance is 1 for all pairs.

When do we have distance 2? Only when two nodes don't share a common neighbor and aren't adjacent.

In example 2: path 1-2-3-4
- 1 and 4 do not share a common neighbor and are not adjacent -> distance 2
- All other pairs have distance 1

So the pattern is:
- Distance(u,v) = 1 if u and v share a common neighbor OR are adjacent in original tree
- Distance(u,v) = 2 otherwise

The set of pairs with distance 1 includes:
1. All edge pairs in original tree
2. All pairs that share a common neighbor (in original tree)

But there's overlap - pairs that are adjacent and also share a common neighbor.

For example, in a path 1-2-3-4, pair (2,3) is adjacent and shares common neighbor 2 with (2,3)? (2,3) shares neighbor 1 with (1,2) and (3,4), but (2,3) themselves share neighbor 2 and 3.

I need to be more systematic.

In the new graph:
- Two nodes are at distance 1 if they are connected directly in the new graph
- The new edges are: original edges + edges between nodes that share a common neighbor

So the new graph has an edge between u and v if:
1. u and v are adjacent in original tree, OR
2. u and v share a common neighbor in original tree

So the pairs with distance 1 are exactly the pairs that have an edge in the new graph.

Total sum of distances = sum over all unordered pairs {u,v} of distance(u,v)

= sum over all {u,v} with edge in new graph of 1 + sum over all {u,v} without edge in new graph of 2

= (number of edges in new graph) + 2 * (total pairs - number of edges in new graph)

So I need:
1. Total pairs = n*(n-1)/2
2. Number of edges in new graph = original edges + edges between nodes that share a common neighbor

Original edges = n-1

Edges between nodes that share a common neighbor = sum over all nodes w of C(deg(w), 2)

So total edges in new graph = (n-1) + sum_w C(deg(w), 2)

Therefore, answer = [(n-1) + sum_w C(deg(w), 2)] + 2 * [n*(n-1)/2 - ((n-1) + sum_w C(deg(w), 2))]
= (n-1) + sum_w C(deg(w), 2) + 2*(n*(n-1)/2) - 2*(n-1) - 2*sum_w C(deg(w), 2)
= (n-1) + sum_w C(deg(w), 2) + n*(n-1) - 2*(n-1) - 2*sum_w C(deg(w), 2)
= [n-1 - 2(n-1) + n(n-1)] + [sum_w C(deg(w), 2) - 2*sum_w C(deg(w), 2)]
= [(n-1)(1 - 2 + n)] - sum_w C(deg(w), 2)
= (n-1)(n-1) - sum_w C(deg(w), 2)
= (n-1)^2 - sum_w C(deg(w), 2)

Let me verify:

Example 1: n=4
(n-1)^2 = 9
sum_w C(deg(w), 2) = C(3,2) + 0 + 0 + 0 = 3
Answer = 9 - 3 = 6 ✓

Example 2: n=4
(n-1)^2 = 9
Degrees: deg(1)=1, deg(2)=2, deg(3)=2, deg(4)=1
C(1,2)=0, C(2,2)=1, C(2,2)=1, C(1,2)=0
Sum = 0+1+1+0 = 2
Answer = 9 - 2 = 7 ✓

Perfect!

## Final Solution

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 1:
    print(0)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Calculate sum of C(deg(w), 2) for all nodes w
    total_sq_sum = 0
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        deg = len(adj[i])
        total_sq_sum += deg * (deg - 1) // 2
    
    # Answer = (n-1)^2 - sum of C(deg(w), 2)
    result = (n-1) * (n-1) - total_sq_sum
    print(result)
```

n = int(input())
if n == 1:
    print(0)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Calculate sum of C(deg(w), 2) for all nodes w
    total_sq_sum = 0
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        deg = len(adj[i])
        total_sq_sum += deg * (deg - 1) // 2
    
    # Answer = (n-1)^2 - sum of C(deg(w), 2)
    result = (n-1) * (n-1) - total_sq_sum
    print(result)
